From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia
Indigenous Polynesian people of New Zealand
This article is about the Māori people of New Zealand. For the Māori people of the Cook Islands, see [Cook Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islanders "Cook Islanders"). For the Maohi people of the Society Islands, see [Tahitians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitians "Tahitians").
Not to be confused with [Murri people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murri_people "Murri people"), [Maouri people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maouri_people "Maouri people"), [Mauri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauri "Mauri"), or [Moriori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriori "Moriori").
Ethnic group
Māori [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/New_Zealand_-_Maori_rowing_-_8444.jpg/250px-New_Zealand_-_Maori_rowing_-_8444.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:New_Zealand_-_Maori_rowing_-_8444.jpg)  
---  
Regions with significant populations  
New Zealand | 978,246 (2023 census)[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-1)  
Australia |  [170,057](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Australians "Māori Australians") (2021 census)[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-2)  
United Kingdom |  [approx. 8,000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealanders_in_the_United_Kingdom#M%C4%81ori "New Zealanders in the United Kingdom") (2000)[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Walrond-3)  
United States |  [3,500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Americans "Māori Americans") (2000)[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-ancestry2000-4)  
Canada | 2,500 (2016)[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-5)  
Other regions |  approx. 8,000[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Walrond-3)  
Languages  
[Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language"), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_English "New Zealand English"), [NZSL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Sign_Language "New Zealand Sign Language")  
Religion  
Mainly [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_New_Zealand "Christianity in New Zealand") or [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_New_Zealand "Irreligion in New Zealand")  
[Rātana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C4%81tana "Rātana")  
[Māori religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_of_M%C4%81ori_people "Religion of Māori people")  
Related ethnic groups  
Other [Polynesian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians "Polynesians"); especially [Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiians "Native Hawaiians"), [Cook Island Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islanders "Cook Islanders"), [Moriori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriori "Moriori"), [Tahitians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitians "Tahitians")

**Māori**[[i]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-14) are the [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Oceania "Indigenous peoples of Oceania") [Polynesian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians "Polynesians") of mainland [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand "New Zealand"). The Māori are descended from East [Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia "Polynesia") settlers who arrived in New Zealand in several waves of [canoe voyages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_migration_canoes "Māori migration canoes") between roughly 1320 and 1350.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Walters_et_al_\(2017\)-15) Over several centuries in isolation, these settlers developed [a distinct culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_culture "Māori culture"), whose language, mythology, crafts, and performing arts evolved independently from those of other eastern Polynesian cultures. Some early Māori moved to the [Chatham Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Islands "Chatham Islands"), where their descendants became New Zealand's other indigenous Polynesian ethnic group, the [Moriori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriori "Moriori").[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-16)[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-King-Moriori-17)[_[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_]
Early contact between Māori and Europeans, starting in the 18th century, ranged from beneficial trade to lethal violence; Māori actively adopted many technologies from the newcomers. With the signing of the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi "Treaty of Waitangi") in 1840, the two cultures coexisted for a generation. Rising tensions over disputed land sales led to conflict in the 1860s, and subsequent land confiscations, which Māori resisted fiercely. After the Treaty was declared a legal nullity in 1877, Māori were [forced to assimilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_assimilation "Forced assimilation") into many aspects of [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture "Western culture"). Social upheaval and epidemics of introduced disease took a devastating toll on the Māori population, which fell dramatically, but began to recover by the beginning of the 20th century. The March 2023 New Zealand census gives the number of people of Māori descent as 978,246 (19.6% of the total population), up from 18.5% in 2018.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-18)[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-19) Of those identifying as Māori at the 2023 census, 366,015 people (41.2%) identified as of sole Māori ethnicity while 409,401 people (46.1%) identified as of both European and Māori ethnicity.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-20)[_[better source needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_]
Efforts have been made, centring on the Treaty of Waitangi, to increase the standing of Māori in wider New Zealand society and achieve [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice "Social justice"). Traditional Māori culture has enjoyed a significant revival, which was further bolstered by a [Māori protest movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_protest_movement "Māori protest movement") that emerged in the 1960s. However, disproportionate numbers of Māori face significant economic and social obstacles, and generally have lower life expectancies and incomes than other New Zealand ethnic groups. They suffer higher levels of crime, health problems, imprisonment, poverty and educational under-achievement. A number of socio-economic initiatives have been instigated with the aim of "[closing the gaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closing_the_gaps "Closing the gaps")" between Māori and other New Zealanders. Political and economic redress for historical grievances is also ongoing (see [Treaty of Waitangi claims and settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi_claims_and_settlements "Treaty of Waitangi claims and settlements")). 
Māori are the second-largest ethnic group in New Zealand, after European New Zealanders (commonly known by the Māori name _[Pākehā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81keh%C4%81 "Pākehā")_). In addition, [more than 170,000 Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Australians "Māori Australians") live in Australia. The [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language") is spoken to some extent by about a fifth of all Māori, representing three per cent of the total population. Māori are active in all spheres of New Zealand culture and society, with independent representation in areas such as media, politics, and sport. 
## Naming and identification
In the [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language"), the word _māori_ means 'normal', 'natural', or 'ordinary'. In legends and oral traditions, the word distinguished ordinary mortal human beings— _tāngata māori_ —from [deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_of_M%C4%81ori_people "Religion of Māori people") and spirits (_wairua_).[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-21)[[ii]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-22) Likewise, _wai māori_ denotes 'fresh water', as opposed to [salt water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_water "Salt water"). There are [cognate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognate "Cognate") words in most [Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_languages "Polynesian languages"),[[iii]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-23) all deriving from Proto-Polynesian *_ma(a)qoli_ , which has the reconstructed meaning 'true, real, genuine'.[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-pollex-24)
Early visitors from Europe to New Zealand generally referred to the indigenous inhabitants as "New Zealanders" or as "natives".[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-25) The Māori used the term _Māori_ to describe themselves in a pan-tribal sense.[[iv]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-26) Māori people often use the term _[tangata whenua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangata_whenua "Tangata whenua")_ (literal meaning, "people of the land") to identify in a way that expresses their relationship with a particular area of land; a tribe may be the _tangata whenua_ in one area, but not in another.[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-27) The term can also refer to the Māori people as a whole in relation to New Zealand (_[Aotearoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aotearoa "Aotearoa")_) as a whole. 
The official definition of Māori for electoral purposes has changed over time. Before 1974, the government required documented ancestry to determine the status of "a Māori person" and only those with at least 50% Māori ancestry were allowed to choose which seats they wished to vote in. The Māori Affairs Amendment Act 1974 changed this, allowing individuals to self-identify as to their cultural identity. Until 1986, the census required at least 50 per cent Māori ancestry to claim Māori affiliation. Currently, in most contexts, authorities require some documentation of ancestry or continuing cultural connection (such as acceptance by others as being of the people); however, there is no minimum ancestry requirement.[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-28)[[v]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-30)
## History
Main article: [Māori history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_history "Māori history")
### Origins in Polynesia
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/First_human_migration_to_New_Zealand.svg/500px-First_human_migration_to_New_Zealand.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:First_human_migration_to_New_Zealand.svg)The Māori settlement of New Zealand represents an end-point of a long chain of [island-hopping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_dispersal "Oceanic dispersal") voyages in the [South Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceania "Oceania").
No credible evidence exists of [pre-Māori settlement of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-M%C4%81ori_settlement_of_New_Zealand "Pre-Māori settlement of New Zealand"); on the other hand, compelling evidence from archaeology, linguistics, and physical anthropology indicates that the first settlers migrated from [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia "Polynesia") and became the Māori.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Walters_et_al_\(2017\)-15)[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-31) Evidence indicates that their ancestry (as part of the larger group of [Austronesian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_peoples "Austronesian peoples")) stretches back 5,000 years, to the [indigenous peoples of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_indigenous_peoples "Taiwanese indigenous peoples"). Polynesian people settled a large area encompassing [Tonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonga "Tonga"), [Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoa "Samoa"), [Tahiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahiti "Tahiti"), [Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Islands "Hawaiian Islands"), [Easter Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island "Easter Island") (Rapa Nui) – and finally New Zealand.[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Wilmshurst_et_al-32)
The date of first arrival and settlement is a matter of debate.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Wilson2020-33) There may have been some exploration and settlement before the eruption of [Mount Tarawera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Tarawera "Mount Tarawera") (c. 1315), based on finds of bones from Polynesian rats and rat-gnawed shells,[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-34) and evidence of widespread forest fires in the decade or so prior.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-35)[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-36) One 2022 study using advanced [radiocarbon technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiocarbon_dating "Radiocarbon dating") suggests that "early Māori settlement happened in the North Island between AD 1250 and AD 1275".[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-37)[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-38) However, a synthesis of archaeological and genetic evidence concludes that, whether or not some settlers arrived before the Tarawera eruption, the main settlement period was in the decades after it, somewhere between 1320 and 1350.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Walters_et_al_\(2017\)-15) This broadly aligns with analyses from Māori oral traditions, which describe the arrival of ancestors in [a number of large ocean-going canoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_migration_canoes "Māori migration canoes") (_[waka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_\(canoe\) "Waka \(canoe\)")_) as a planned mass migration c. 1350.[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-39)[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-40)
There is definitive archaeological evidence of brief settlement as far south as [Enderby Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enderby_Island "Enderby Island"),[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-41) but there is no evidence of travel further south into Antarctic waters despite occasional speculation.[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-42)
### Early history
Further information: [Archaeology of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeology_of_New_Zealand "Archaeology of New Zealand")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Early_Maori_objects_from_Wairau_Bar%2C_Canterbury_Museum%2C_2016-01-27.jpg/330px-Early_Maori_objects_from_Wairau_Bar%2C_Canterbury_Museum%2C_2016-01-27.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Early_Maori_objects_from_Wairau_Bar,_Canterbury_Museum,_2016-01-27.jpg)Early Archaic period artefacts from the [Wairau Bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wairau_Bar "Wairau Bar") archaeological site, on display at the [Canterbury Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canterbury_Museum,_Christchurch "Canterbury Museum, Christchurch") in Christchurch
The earliest period of Māori settlement, which has been called the "Colonisation phase",[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-43) "East Polynesian" phase,[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-44) the "Archaic" period, and the "Moahunter" period,[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-45) dates from the time of arrival to c. 1500. The early Māori diet included an abundance of [moa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moa "Moa") and other large birds and fur seals that had never been hunted before. This Archaic period is known for its distinctive "reel necklaces",[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-NgaKakano-46) and also remarkable for the lack of weapons and fortifications typical of the later "Classic" Māori.[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-47) The best-known and most extensively studied Archaic site, at [Wairau Bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wairau_Bar "Wairau Bar") in the South Island,[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-48) shows evidence of occupation from the early 13th century to the early 15th century.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-McFadgen_and_Adds_\(2018\)-49) It is the only known New Zealand archaeological site containing the bones of people who were born elsewhere.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-McFadgen_and_Adds_\(2018\)-49)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Model_Of_Maori_Pa_On_Headland.jpg/250px-Model_Of_Maori_Pa_On_Headland.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Model_Of_Maori_Pa_On_Headland.jpg)Model of a _[pā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81 "Pā")_ (hillfort) built on a headland. _Pā_ proliferated as competition and warfare increased among a growing population.
Factors that operated in the transition to the Classic period (the culture at the time of European contact) include a significantly [cooler period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Ice_Age "Little Ice Age") from 1500,[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-atholl-50) and the extinction of the [moa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moa#Relationship_with_humans "Moa") and of other food species.[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-51)[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-52)[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-53)[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-54)[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-55)
The Classic period is characterised by finely made _[pounamu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pounamu "Pounamu")_ (greenstone) weapons and ornaments, elaborately carved [war canoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_taua "Waka taua") and _[wharenui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharenui "Wharenui")_ (meeting houses).[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-neich-56) Māori lived in autonomous settlements in extended [hapū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hap%C5%AB "Hapū") groups descended from common [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi "Iwi") ancestors. The settlements had farmed areas and food sources for hunting, fishing and gathering. Fortified [pā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81 "Pā") were built at strategic locations due to occasional warfare over wrongdoings or resources; this practice varied over different locations throughout New Zealand, with more populations in the far north.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Wilson2020-33)[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-57)[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-58) There is a stereotype that Māori were 'natural warriors'; however, warfare and associated practices like [cannibalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_cannibalism "Human cannibalism") were not a dominant part of Māori culture.[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-59)[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-60)[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-61)
Around the 15th century,[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-62) a group of Māori migrated east to the [Chatham Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Islands "Chatham Islands") and developed into a people known as the [Moriori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriori "Moriori"),[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-63) with [pacifism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifism "Pacifism") a key part of their culture.[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-King-Moriori-17)
### Contact with Europeans
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Gilsemans_1642.jpg/330px-Gilsemans_1642.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gilsemans_1642.jpg)A drawing from [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman "Abel Tasman")'s [travel journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_journal "Travel journal") of the first encounter between Europeans and Māori, in 1642
The first European explorers of New Zealand were [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman "Abel Tasman"), who arrived in 1642, Captain [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook "James Cook"), in 1769, and [Marion du Fresne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc-Joseph_Marion_du_Fresne "Marc-Joseph Marion du Fresne") in 1772. Initial contact between Māori and Europeans proved problematic and sometimes fatal, with Tasman having four of his men killed and probably killing at least one Māori, without ever landing.[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-64) Cook's men shot at least eight Māori within three days of his first landing,[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-65)[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-66) although he later had good relations with Māori. Three years later, after a promising start, du Fresne and 26 men of his crew were killed. From the 1780s, Māori also increasingly encountered European and American [sealers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_hunting "Seal hunting"), [whalers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whaling "Whaling") and Christian [missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionaries "Missionaries"). Relations were mostly peaceful, although marred by several further violent incidents, the worst of which was the [_Boyd_ massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boyd_massacre "Boyd massacre") in 1807 and subsequent revenge attacks.[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-67)
[European settlement in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_settlers_in_New_Zealand "European settlers in New Zealand") began in the early 19th century, leading to an extensive sharing of culture and ideas. Many Māori valued Europeans, whom they called "_[Pākehā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81keh%C4%81 "Pākehā")_ ", as a means to acquire Western knowledge and technology. Māori quickly adopted writing as a means of sharing ideas, and many of their oral stories and poems were converted to the written form.[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-68) The introduction of the [potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato "Potato") revolutionised agriculture, and the acquisition of [muskets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket "Musket")[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Old_New_Zealand-69) by Māori _iwi_ led to a period of particularly bloody [intertribal warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic_warfare "Endemic warfare") known as the [Musket Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket_Wars "Musket Wars"), in which many groups were decimated and others driven from their traditional territory.[[65]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-70) The pacifist [Moriori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriori "Moriori") in the Chatham Islands similarly suffered massacre and subjugation in an invasion by some Taranaki _iwi_.[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-71) At the same time, the Māori suffered high mortality rates from Eurasian infectious diseases, such as [influenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Influenza "Influenza"), [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox "Smallpox") and [measles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Measles "Measles"), which killed an estimated 10 to 50 per cent of Māori.[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-72)[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-73)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Reconstruction_of_the_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Waitangi%2C_Marcus_King_%2816044258961%29.jpg/250px-Reconstruction_of_the_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Waitangi%2C_Marcus_King_%2816044258961%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Reconstruction_of_the_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Waitangi,_Marcus_King_\(16044258961\).jpg)Depiction of the signing of the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi "Treaty of Waitangi") in 1840, bringing New Zealand and the Māori into the British Empire
By 1839, estimates placed the number of Europeans living in New Zealand as high as 2,000,[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-74) and the [British Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown "The Crown") acceded to repeated requests from missionaries and some Māori chiefs (_[rangatira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangatira "Rangatira")_) to intervene. The British government sent Royal Navy Captain [William Hobson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hobson "William Hobson") to negotiate a treaty between the British Crown and the Māori, which became known as the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi "Treaty of Waitangi"). In 1840, starting on 6 February at Waitangi and by the end of the year at about 50 other events around the country, this treaty was signed by the Crown and over 500 [rangatira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangatira "Rangatira") Māori.[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-75)[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-76) The Treaty gave Māori the rights of [British subjects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_nationality_law "British nationality law") and guaranteed Māori property rights and tribal autonomy, in return for accepting British [sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereignty "Sovereignty") and the annexation of New Zealand as a colony in the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire").[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-77) However, disputes continue over aspects of the Treaty of Waitangi, including wording differences in the two versions (in English and Māori), as well as misunderstandings of different cultural concepts; notably, the Māori version did not cede sovereignty to the British Crown.[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-78) In [an 1877 court case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wi_Parata_v_Bishop_of_Wellington "Wi Parata v Bishop of Wellington") the Treaty was declared a "simple nullity" on the grounds that the signatories had been "primitive barbarians".[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-79)[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-80)
Nevertheless, relations between Māori and Pākehā (New Zealand Europeans) during the early colonial period were largely peaceful. Many Māori groups set up substantial businesses, supplying food and other products for domestic and overseas markets. When violence did break out, as in the [Wairau Affray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wairau_Affray "Wairau Affray"), [Flagstaff War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flagstaff_War "Flagstaff War"), [Hutt Valley Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutt_Valley_Campaign "Hutt Valley Campaign") and [Wanganui Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanganui_Campaign "Wanganui Campaign"), it was generally limited and concluded with a peace treaty. However, by the 1860s rising settler numbers and tensions over disputed land purchases led to the later [New Zealand wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_wars "New Zealand wars"), fought by the colonial government against numerous Māori _iwi_ using local and British Imperial troops, and some allied _iwi_. These conflicts resulted in the colonial government [confiscating tracts of Māori land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_land_confiscations "New Zealand land confiscations") as punishment for what were called "rebellions". Pākehā settlers would occupy the confiscated land.[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-81) Several minor conflicts arose after the wars, including the incident at [Parihaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parihaka "Parihaka") in 1881 and the [Dog Tax War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog_Tax_War "Dog Tax War") from 1897 to 1898. The [Native Land Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Land_Court "Native Land Court") was established to transfer Māori land from communal ownership into individual title as a means to assimilation and to facilitate greater sales to European settlers.[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-82)
### Decline and revival
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/E_003261_E_Maoris_in_North_Africa_July_1941.jpg/250px-E_003261_E_Maoris_in_North_Africa_July_1941.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:E_003261_E_Maoris_in_North_Africa_July_1941.jpg)Members of the [28th Māori Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Battalion "Māori Battalion") performing a [haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka "Haka") (dance) in 1941
By the late 19th century, a widespread belief existed amongst both [Pākehā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81keh%C4%81 "Pākehā") and Māori that the Māori population would cease to exist as a separate race or culture, and become assimilated into the European population.[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-83) From the late 19th to the mid-20th century various laws, policies, and practices were instituted in New Zealand society with the effect of inducing Māori to conform to Pākehā norms; notable among these are the [Tohunga Suppression Act 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tohunga_Suppression_Act_1907 "Tohunga Suppression Act 1907") and the suppression of the Māori language by schools,[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-84) often enforced with corporal punishment.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-85) In the 1896 census, New Zealand had a Māori population of 42,113, by which time Europeans numbered more than 700,000.[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-86)
The rapid decline did not continue and the Māori population continued to recover in the 20th century. Influential Māori politicians such as [James Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Carroll_\(New_Zealand_politician\) "James Carroll \(New Zealand politician\)"), [Āpirana Ngata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80pirana_Ngata "Āpirana Ngata"), [Te Rangi Hīroa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Rangi_H%C4%ABroa "Te Rangi Hīroa") and [Māui Pōmare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ui_P%C5%8Dmare "Māui Pōmare") aimed to revitalise the Māori people after the devastation of the previous century. They believed the future path called for a degree of [assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation "Cultural assimilation"),[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-87) with Māori adopting European practices such as [Western medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine#Modern "Medicine"), while also retaining traditional cultural practices. Māori also fought during both World Wars in specialised battalions (the [Māori Pioneer Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_\(M%C4%81ori\)_Pioneer_Battalion "New Zealand \(Māori\) Pioneer Battalion") in WWI and the [28th (Māori) Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Battalion "Māori Battalion") in WWII). Māori were also badly hit by the [1918 influenza epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1918_influenza_epidemic "1918 influenza epidemic"), with death rates for Māori being five to seven times higher than for Pākehā.[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-88)[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-89)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Whina_Cooper_in_Hamilton.jpg/250px-Whina_Cooper_in_Hamilton.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Whina_Cooper_in_Hamilton.jpg)[Whina Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whina_Cooper "Whina Cooper") leading the Māori Land March in 1975, seeking redress for historical grievances
Since the 1960s, Māoridom has undergone a [cultural revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_renaissance "Māori renaissance")[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-90) concurrent with activism for social justice and a [protest movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_protest_movement "Māori protest movement").[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-91) _[Kōhanga reo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dhanga_reo "Kōhanga reo")_ (Māori language pre-schools) were established in 1982 to promote Māori language use and halt the decline in its use.[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-92) Two Māori language television channels broadcast content in the Māori language,[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-M%C4%81oriTVlaunch-93)[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-94) while words such as "_[kia ora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kia_ora "Kia ora")_ " have entered widespread use in [New Zealand English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_English "New Zealand English").[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-95)
Government recognition of the growing political power of Māori and political activism have led to limited redress for historic land confiscations. In 1975, the Crown set up the [Waitangi Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Tribunal "Waitangi Tribunal") to investigate historical grievances,[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-96) and since the 1990s the New Zealand government has negotiated and finalised [treaty settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi_claims_and_settlements "Treaty of Waitangi claims and settlements") with many _iwi_ across New Zealand. By June 2008, the government had provided over NZ$900 million in settlements, much of it in the form of land deals.[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-QuarterlyReport-97) There is a growing Māori leadership who are using these settlements as an investment platform for economic development.[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-HeraldJames-98)
Despite a growing acceptance of Māori culture in wider New Zealand society, treaty settlements have generated significant controversy. Some Māori have argued that the settlements occur at a level of between one and two-and-a-half cents on the dollar of the value of the confiscated lands, and do not represent adequate redress. Conversely, some non-Māori denounce the settlements and socioeconomic initiatives as amounting to race-based preferential treatment.[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-WaitangiDebateTVNZ-99) Both of these sentiments were expressed during the [New Zealand foreshore and seabed controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_foreshore_and_seabed_controversy "New Zealand foreshore and seabed controversy") in 2004.[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-100)[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-101)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Tawhiao_Maori_King_1880s.jpg/250px-Tawhiao_Maori_King_1880s.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tawhiao_Maori_King_1880s.jpg)[Tāwhiao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%81whiao "Tāwhiao"), the second Māori king, reigned from 1860 to 1894.
The [Māori King movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_King_movement "Māori King movement"), called the _Kīngitanga_[[vi]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-104) in Māori, arose among some of the Māori [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi "Iwi") in the central North Island in the 1850s, to establish a role similar in status to that of the monarch of the British colonists, as a way of halting the alienation of Māori land.[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-nzha-105) The Māori monarch operates in a non-constitutional capacity with no legal or judicial power within the New Zealand government. Reigning monarchs retain the position of [paramount chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount_chief "Paramount chief") of several iwi[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Foster-106) and wield some power over these, especially within [Tainui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tainui "Tainui"). 
The current Māori monarch, [Nga wai hono i te po](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nga_wai_hono_i_te_po "Nga wai hono i te po"), was [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elective_monarchy "Elective monarchy") in 2024.[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-p198-107) Her official residence is Tūrongo House at [Tūrangawaewae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%ABrangawaewae "Tūrangawaewae") [marae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marae "Marae") in the town of [Ngāruawāhia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ruaw%C4%81hia "Ngāruawāhia"). She is the eighth monarch since the position was created and is the continuation of a dynasty that reaches back to the inaugural king, [Pōtatau Te Wherowhero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C5%8Dtatau_Te_Wherowhero "Pōtatau Te Wherowhero"). 
The [movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_movement "Political movement") arose among a group of central North Island iwi in the 1850s as a means of attaining Māori unity to halt the alienation of land at a time of rapid population growth by European colonists.[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-nzha-105) The movement sought to establish a monarch who could claim status similar to that of [Queen Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria "Queen Victoria") and thus allow Māori to deal with _[Pākehā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81keh%C4%81 "Pākehā")_ (Europeans) on equal footing. It took on the appearance of an alternative government with its own flag, newspaper, bank, councillors, magistrates and law enforcement. But it was viewed by the colonial government as a challenge to the supremacy of the [British monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_the_United_Kingdom "Monarchy of the United Kingdom"), leading in turn to the 1863 [invasion of Waikato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Waikato "Invasion of Waikato"), which was partly motivated by a drive to neutralise the Kīngitanga's power and influence. Following their defeat at Ōrākau in 1864, Kīngitanga forces withdrew into the Ngāti Maniapoto tribal region of the North Island that became known as the [King Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Country "King Country").[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-walkerstruggle-108)[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-DaltonWarPolitics-109)[_[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_] The Māori monarch's influence has not been as strong as it could be, partially due to the lack of affiliation to the Kīngitanga of key iwi, most notably [Tūhoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81i_T%C5%ABhoe "Ngāi Tūhoe"), [Ngāti Porou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Porou "Ngāti Porou"), and the largest iwi of all, [Ngāpuhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81puhi "Ngāpuhi").[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-110)
## Demographics
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Maori_ethnicity_declared_in_2018.png/330px-Maori_ethnicity_declared_in_2018.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Maori_ethnicity_declared_in_2018.png)Māori in New Zealand in 2018 [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/M%C4%81ori_New_Zealanders_population_pyramid_in_2018.svg/250px-M%C4%81ori_New_Zealanders_population_pyramid_in_2018.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:M%C4%81ori_New_Zealanders_population_pyramid_in_2018.svg)Māori New Zealanders population pyramid in 2018
Under the Māori Affairs Amendment Act 1974, a Māori is defined as "a person of the Māori race of New Zealand; and includes any descendant of such a person".[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-111) The Māori population around the late 18th century was estimated by James Cook at 100,000. Historian Michael King suggests a slightly higher figure of 110,000 is more likely.[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-112) Their numbers declined during the 19th century, to as low as 42,000; the decline has been attributed to the impact of European colonisation, including new diseases.[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-113) Thereafter the population grew rapidly. 
There were 887,493 people identifying as being part of the Māori ethnic group at the [2023 New Zealand census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_census "2023 New Zealand census"), making up 17.8% of New Zealand's population.[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-2023_census-114) This is an increase of 111,657 people (14.4%) since the [2018 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_New_Zealand_census "2018 New Zealand census"), and an increase of 288,891 people (48.3%) since the [2013 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_New_Zealand_census "2013 New Zealand census"). The large increase between the 2013 and 2018 census was mainly due to [Statistics New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistics_New_Zealand "Statistics New Zealand") starting to add ethnicity data from other sources (previous censuses, administrative data, and imputation) to the census data to reduce the number of non-responses.[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-115)
The median age of Māori was 26.8 years, compared with 38.1 years for New Zealand as a whole. 262,422 people (29.6%) were aged under 15 years, 223,860 (25.2%) were 15 to 29, 336,486 (37.9%) were 30 to 64, and 64,725 (7.3%) were 65 or older. 
In terms of population distribution, 753,384 (84.9%) Māori lived in the North Island at the 2023 census and 133,656 (15.1%) lived in the South Island. Five districts had a [majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_minority "Majority minority") Māori population: [Chatham Islands territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Islands "Chatham Islands") (68.6%), [Wairoa district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wairoa_District "Wairoa District") (68.5%), [Ōpōtiki district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%8Cp%C5%8Dtiki_District "Ōpōtiki District") (66.2%), [Kawerau district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawerau_District "Kawerau District") (63.2%) and [Gisborne district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gisborne_District "Gisborne District") (54.8%). The [Upper Harbour local board area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Harbour_Local_Board "Upper Harbour Local Board") in Auckland has the lowest concentration of Māori people at 6.1%, followed by the [Devonport-Takapuna local board area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devonport-Takapuna "Devonport-Takapuna") (6.2%) and the [Howick local board area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howick_\(local_board_area\) "Howick \(local board area\)") (6.3%), The [Queenstown-Lakes District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queenstown-Lakes_District "Queenstown-Lakes District") had the lowest concentration of Māori outside Auckland at 6.4%.[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-2023_census-114)
Of those identifying as Māori at the 2023 census, 366,015 people (41.2%) identified as of sole Māori ethnicity while 409,401 people (46.1%) identified as of both European and Māori ethnicity, due to the high rate of intermarriage between the two ethnicities.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-116)
The largest _iwi_ by population at the 2013 census was [Ngāpuhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81puhi "Ngāpuhi") (125,601), followed by [Ngāti Porou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Porou "Ngāti Porou") (71,049), [Ngāi Tahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81i_Tahu "Ngāi Tahu") (54,819) and [Waikato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waikato_\(iwi\) "Waikato \(iwi\)") (40,083). However, over 110,000 people of Māori descent could not identify their _iwi_.[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Quickstats-117)
Outside of New Zealand, a large Māori population exists in Australia. There were 170,057 Australians identifying as Māori at the [2021 Australian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Australian_census "2021 Australian census"), with 65,031 living in [Queensland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensland "Queensland"), 39,714 living in [New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_South_Wales "New South Wales") and 31,044 living in [Western Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Australia "Western Australia").[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Hamer-118) Smaller communities also exist in the United Kingdom (approx. 8,000), the United States (up to 3,500) and Canada (approx. 2,805).[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Walrond-3)[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-uscb-119)[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-statcan-120)
## Culture
Main article: [Māori culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_culture "Māori culture")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/TamatekapuaMeetingHouse.jpg/250px-TamatekapuaMeetingHouse.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TamatekapuaMeetingHouse.jpg) _[Wharenui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharenui "Wharenui")_ (meeting house) at [Ōhinemutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohinemutu "Ohinemutu") village, [Rotorua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotorua "Rotorua") (_[tekoteko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tekoteko "Tekoteko")_ on the top)
Māori culture forms a distinctive part of [New Zealand culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_New_Zealand "Culture of New Zealand") and, due to a large diaspora and the incorporation of Māori motifs into [popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_culture "Popular culture"), is found throughout the world.[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-121)[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-122) Contemporary Māori culture comprises traditional as well as 20th-century influences. 
### Traditional culture
Archaeological record indicates a gradual evolution of culture.[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-123) In the course of a few centuries, the growing population led to competition for resources and an increase in warfare and an increased frequency of fortified [pā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81 "Pā"). Various systems also arose aimed to conserve resources; most of these, such as _[tapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapu_\(Polynesian_culture\) "Tapu \(Polynesian culture\)")_ and _[rāhui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahui "Rahui")_ , used religious or supernatural threats to discourage people from taking species at particular seasons or from specified areas. 
Warfare between tribes was common, and Māori would sometimes eat their conquered enemies or enslave them.[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-124) Performing arts such as the [haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka "Haka") developed from their Polynesian roots, as did carving and weaving. Regional dialects arose, with differences in vocabulary and in the pronunciation of some words but the language retained enough similarities to other [Eastern Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Polynesian_languages "Eastern Polynesian languages") for [Tupaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupaia_\(navigator\) "Tupaia \(navigator\)"), the [Tahitian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitians "Tahitians") navigator on [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook "James Cook")'s first voyage in the region to act as an interpreter between Māori and the crew of the _[Endeavour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Endeavour "HMS Endeavour")_. 
### Belief and religion
Main articles: [Māori mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_mythology "Māori mythology") and [Māori religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_religion "Māori religion")
Religious affiliation[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-2018_Census-125) |  |   
---|---|---  
No religion  |  | 53.5%  
Christian |  | 29.9%  
Māori religions |  | 7.7%  
Other religion  |  | 1.4%  
Buddhism |  | 0.2%  
Islam |  | 0.1%  
Hinduism |  | 0.1%  
Traditional Māori beliefs have their origins in [Polynesian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_culture "Polynesian culture"). Concepts such as _[tapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapu_\(Polynesian_culture\) "Tapu \(Polynesian culture\)")_ (sacred), _[noa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapu_\(Polynesian_culture\)#Noa "Tapu \(Polynesian culture\)")_ (non-sacred), _[mana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mana_\(Oceanian_mythology\) "Mana \(Oceanian mythology\)")_ (authority/prestige) and _wairua_ (spirit) governed everyday Māori living, and there are also many [Māori deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_M%C4%81ori_deities "List of Māori deities"). Today, some Māori follow a variety of Christian faiths such as [Presbyterianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism "Presbyterianism"), [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints "The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints"), Māori Christian groups such as [Rātana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C4%81tana "Rātana") and [Ringatū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ringat%C5%AB "Ringatū"),[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-religion-126) and also [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic "Catholic"), [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican "Anglican") and [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist "Methodist") denominations.[[121]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-127)[[122]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-128) At the [2018 New Zealand census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_New_Zealand_census "2018 New Zealand census"), 7.7 per cent of Māori were affiliated with Māori religions, beliefs, and philosophies; 29.9 per cent with Christian denominations and 53.5 per cent of Māori claimed [no religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_New_Zealand "Irreligion in New Zealand"). Proportions of Christian and irreligious Māori are comparable with European New Zealanders.[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-2018_Census-125)
Many Māori people observe spiritual traditions such as _[tapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapu_\(Polynesian_culture\) "Tapu \(Polynesian culture\)")_ and _[noa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapu_\(Polynesian_culture\)#Noa "Tapu \(Polynesian culture\)")_. Certain objects, areas, or buildings are _tapu_ (spiritually restricted), and must be made _noa_ (unrestricted) by ceremonial action.[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-129) It is common practice, for instance, to remove one's shoes before entering a _wharenui_ (meeting-house), a token of respect for the ancestors who are represented and spiritually present within the _wharenui_.[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-130) Another spiritual ritual is _hurihanga takapau_ (purification), practised when fishing to ensure there is no _tapu_ on the fish.[[125]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-131)
### Performing arts
Cultural performance of _[waiata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_music "Māori music")_ (song), _[haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka "Haka")_ (dance), _tauparapara_ (chants) and _mōteatea_ (poetry) are used by Māori to express and pass on knowledge and understanding about history, communities, and relationships.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-132) _[Kapa haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapa_haka "Kapa haka")_ is a Māori performance art[[127]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-133) believed to have originated with the legendary figure [Tinirau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinirau "Tinirau"). It was performed for tourists following European contact, starting in the 1880s; this sometimes involved adaptations to make it more familiar to European audiences.[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-V.Smith-2014-134) It was used in the First World War to raise money for the Maori Soldiers' Fund encouraged by [Āpirana Ngata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80pirana_Ngata "Āpirana Ngata").[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-V.Smith-2014-134) A _haka_ is often performed in a _[pōwhiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C5%8Dwhiri "Pōwhiri")_ (welcoming ceremony).[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Keane-135)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Te_Matatini_2015_027.jpg/250px-Te_Matatini_2015_027.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Te_Matatini_2015_027.jpg)[Te Matatini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Matatini "Te Matatini") 2015 in [Hagley Park, Christchurch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagley_Park,_Christchurch "Hagley Park, Christchurch")
Since 1972, there has been a regular national _kapa haka_ competition, the [Te Matatini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Matatini "Te Matatini") National Festival, organised by the Aotearoa Traditional Māori Performing Arts Society. There are _kapa haka_ groups in schools, tertiary institutions, and workplaces, and it is performed at tourist venues across the country.[[130]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Diamond-136)[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Swarbrick-137)
_[Whare tapere](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Whare_tapere&action=edit&redlink=1 "Whare tapere \(page does not exist\)")_ (entertainment houses) were a site of story-telling, dance, and puppetry in pre-European Māori culture.[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-138)[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-139) Māori theatre and contemporary dance flourished in the 1970s and 1980s with groups such as [Te Ohu Whakaari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Ohu_Whakaari "Te Ohu Whakaari"), Te Ika a Maui Players and [Taki Rua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taki_Rua "Taki Rua"). Contemporary Māori stage writers, actors and directors include [George Henare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Henare "George Henare"), [Riwia Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riwia_Brown "Riwia Brown"), [Hone Kouka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hone_Kouka "Hone Kouka"), [Nancy Brunning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Brunning "Nancy Brunning"), [Jim Moriarty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Moriarty "Jim Moriarty"), [Briar Grace-Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Briar_Grace-Smith "Briar Grace-Smith"), and many others.[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-140) Contemporary performing arts include theatre companies Taki Rua,[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-141) Tawata Productions who run an annual playwriting festival for indigenous writers called Breaking Ground,[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-142)[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-143) and dance companies, [Atamira Dance Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atamira_dance_company "Atamira dance company") and Okareka Dance Company.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-144) In [Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland "Auckland") there is Te Pou, a kaupapa Māori performing arts venue that develops and partners with Māori theatre makers.[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-145)
Traditional Māori instruments are [taonga pūoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taonga_p%C5%ABoro "Taonga pūoro"). They fulfilled various roles including storytelling, religious traditions and also daily functions such as the beginning of a new day.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-146) Taonga pūoro fall into two areas, melodic instruments such as the flute and rhythmic instruments such as [poi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poi_\(performance_art\) "Poi \(performance art\)") "balls of dried flax on string that are swung and tapped".[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-147)
Prominent Māori music artists and groups include [Stan Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Walker "Stan Walker"), [Maisey Rika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maisey_Rika "Maisey Rika"), [Young Sid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Sid "Young Sid"), [William Singe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Singe "William Singe"), [Teeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teeks "Teeks"), [Ria Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ria_Hall "Ria Hall"), [Rob Ruha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Ruha "Rob Ruha"), [Pieter T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pieter_T "Pieter T"),[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] [Alien Weaponry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien_Weaponry "Alien Weaponry"), [Sons of Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Zion "Sons of Zion"), [1814](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1814_\(band\) "1814 \(band\)")[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] and [Maimoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimoa "Maimoa").

### Literature and media
See also: [New Zealand art § Prehistoric art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_art#Prehistoric_art "New Zealand art"), and [New Zealand art § Traditional Māori art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_art#Traditional_M%C4%81ori_art "New Zealand art")
Like other cultures, oral folklore was used by Māori to preserve their stories and beliefs through many centuries. In the 19th century, European-style literacy was brought to the Māori, which led to Māori history documentation in books, novels and later television. Māori language use began to decline in the 20th century with English as the language through which Māori literature became widespread. 
Notable Māori novelists include [Patricia Grace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricia_Grace "Patricia Grace"), [Witi Ihimaera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witi_Ihimaera "Witi Ihimaera") and [Alan Duff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Duff "Alan Duff"). _[Once Were Warriors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Once_Were_Warriors_\(film\) "Once Were Warriors \(film\)")_ , a 1994 film adapted from a 1990 [novel of the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Once_Were_Warriors "Once Were Warriors") by Alan Duff, brought the plight of some urban Māori to a wide audience. It was the highest-grossing film in New Zealand until 2006,[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-148)[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-149) and received international acclaim, winning several international film prizes.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-150) While some Māori feared that viewers would consider the violent male characters an accurate portrayal of Māori men, most critics praised it as exposing the raw side of [domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence "Domestic violence").[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-151)
Other major films with Māori themes or subjects include [_Utu_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utu_\(film\) "Utu \(film\)") (1983), _[The Piano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Piano "The Piano")_ (1993), _[Whale Rider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whale_Rider "Whale Rider")_ (2002), _[River Queen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Queen "River Queen")_ (2005), _[Boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_\(2010_film\) "Boy \(2010 film\)")_ (2010), _[Hunt for the Wilderpeople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunt_for_the_Wilderpeople "Hunt for the Wilderpeople")_ (2016) and [_Muru_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muru_\(film\) "Muru \(film\)") (2022). _[The Maori Merchant of Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Maori_Merchant_of_Venice "The Maori Merchant of Venice")_ (2002) was notable as a complete Māori language translation and performance of Shakespeare's _[The Merchant of Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Merchant_of_Venice "The Merchant of Venice")_. 
Prominent Māori actors include [Temuera Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temuera_Morrison "Temuera Morrison"), [Cliff Curtis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cliff_Curtis "Cliff Curtis"), [Jemaine Clement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemaine_Clement "Jemaine Clement"), [Lawrence Makoare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Makoare "Lawrence Makoare"), [Miriama Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miriama_Smith "Miriama Smith"), [Manu Bennett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manu_Bennett "Manu Bennett"), [Keisha Castle-Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keisha_Castle-Hughes "Keisha Castle-Hughes"), [James Rolleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Rolleston "James Rolleston"), [Rena Owen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rena_Owen "Rena Owen"), [Shavaughn Ruakere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shavaughn_Ruakere "Shavaughn Ruakere") and [Julian Dennison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_Dennison "Julian Dennison"). In most cases their roles in [Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood_\(film_industry\) "Hollywood \(film industry\)") productions have them portraying ethnic groups other than Māori. 
In the 2010s Māori actor-director [Taika Waititi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taika_Waititi "Taika Waititi") rose to global fame directing the [Marvel Cinematic Universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marvel_Cinematic_Universe "Marvel Cinematic Universe") film _[Thor: Ragnarok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thor:_Ragnarok "Thor: Ragnarok")_ (2017),[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-152) and the [Academy Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award "Academy Award")-winning _[Jojo Rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jojo_Rabbit "Jojo Rabbit")_ (2019),[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-153) in which he played [Adolf Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler "Adolf Hitler") in a supporting role. Waititi's previous films _[Boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_\(2010_film\) "Boy \(2010 film\)")_[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-screen-154) and _[Hunt for the Wilderpeople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunt_for_the_Wilderpeople "Hunt for the Wilderpeople")_ ,[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-155) both feature young Māori protagonists.
