### Sport
See also: [List of New Zealand Māori sportspeople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Zealand_M%C4%81ori_sportspeople "List of New Zealand Māori sportspeople")
Māori participate fully in [New Zealand's sporting culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_New_Zealand "Sport in New Zealand"), and are well-represented in rugby union, rugby league and netball teams at all levels. Individual Māori players are members of national sports teams, and there are dedicated Māori [rugby union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_All_Blacks "Māori All Blacks"), [rugby league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_M%C4%81ori_rugby_league_team "New Zealand Māori rugby league team") and [cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_M%C4%81ori_cricket_team "New Zealand Māori cricket team") teams that play in international competitions. 
At the [2016 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics "2016 Summer Olympics") in Rio de Janeiro, 41 of the 199 competitors (20.5 per cent) were of Māori descent in the [New Zealand delegation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_at_the_2016_Summer_Olympics "New Zealand at the 2016 Summer Olympics"), with the rugby sevens squads alone having 17 Māori competitors (out of 24). There were also three competitors of Māori descent in the [Australian delegation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_at_the_2016_Summer_Olympics "Australia at the 2016 Summer Olympics").[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-156)
[![Rugby team wearing all black, facing the camera, knees bent, and facing toward a team wearing white](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Haka_2006.jpg/250px-Haka_2006.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Haka_2006.jpg)A [haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka_\(sports\) "Haka \(sports\)") performed by the [national rugby union team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_national_rugby_union_team "New Zealand national rugby union team") before a game [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Maori_team_2008_RLWC.jpg/250px-Maori_team_2008_RLWC.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Maori_team_2008_RLWC.jpg)[New Zealand Māori rugby league team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_M%C4%81ori_rugby_league_team "New Zealand Māori rugby league team") vs [Aboriginal Dreamtime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_dreamtime_team "Aboriginal dreamtime team") match at 2008 Rugby League world cup
The [New Zealand national rugby union team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_national_rugby_union_team "New Zealand national rugby union team") and many other [New Zealand sports people perform a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka_in_sports "Haka in sports") _[haka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haka_in_sports "Haka in sports")_ , a traditional Māori challenge, before events.[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-157)[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-158)
[Kī-o-rahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%AB-o-rahi "Kī-o-rahi") and [Tapawai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapawai "Tapawai") are two [ball sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ball_game "Ball game") of Māori origin. Kī-o-rahi received an unexpected boost when [McDonald's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonald%27s "McDonald's") chose it to represent New Zealand.[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-159) [Waka ama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_ama "Waka ama") (outrigger canoeing) has also experienced a resurgence of interest in New Zealand since the 1980s.[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-160)
## Language
Main articles: [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language") and [Māori language revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language_revival "Māori language revival")
[![Map of New Zealand showing the percentage of people in each census area unit who speak Māori. Areas of the North Island exhibit the highest Māori proficiency.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/TeReoMaori2013.png/250px-TeReoMaori2013.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TeReoMaori2013.png)Speakers of Māori according to the 2013 census[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-161)
The Māori language, also known as _te reo Māori_ (pronounced [ˈmaːoɾi,) or simply _Te Reo_ ("the language"), has the status of an official language. Linguists classify it within the Eastern Polynesian languages as being closely related to [Cook Islands Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islands_M%C4%81ori "Cook Islands Māori"), [Tuamotuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuamotuan_language "Tuamotuan language") and [Tahitian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitian_language "Tahitian language"). Before European contact Māori did not have a written language and "important information such as _whakapapa_ was memorised and passed down verbally through the generations".[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-162) Māori were familiar with the concept of maps and when interacting with missionaries in 1815 could draw accurate maps of their _[rohe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohe "Rohe")_ (_iwi_ boundaries), onto paper, that were the equal of European maps. Missionaries surmised that Māori had traditionally drawn maps on sand or other natural materials.[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-163)
From about 1890, Māori [members of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Members_of_Parliament "Members of Parliament") realised the importance of English literacy to Māori and insisted that all Māori children be taught in English.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] Missionaries, who still ran many Māori schools, had been teaching exclusively in Māori but the Māori MPs insisted this should stop. However attendance at school for many Māori was intermittent. In many areas of New Zealand, Māori lost its role as a living community language used by significant numbers of people in the [post-war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-war "Post-war") years. In tandem with calls for sovereignty and for the righting of social injustices from the 1970s onwards, New Zealand schools now teach Māori culture and language as an option, and pre-school _[kohanga reo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_nest "Language nest")_ ("language-nests") have started, which teach _[tamariki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamariki "Tamariki")_ (young children) exclusively in Māori. These now[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=M%C4%81ori_people&action=edit) extend right through secondary schools (_kura tuarua_). Most preschool centres teach basics such as colours, numerals and greetings in Māori songs and chants.[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-164)
[Māori Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Television "Māori Television"), a government-funded channel committed to broadcasting primarily in Te Reo, began in March 2004.[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-M%C4%81oriTVlaunch-93) The 1996 census reported 160,000 Māori speakers.[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-quickstats-165) At the time of the 2013 census 125,352 Māori (21.3 per cent) reported a conversational level of [proficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_proficiency "Language proficiency").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-166)
## Social organisation
### Historical development
Polynesian settlers in New Zealand developed a distinct society over several hundred years. Social groups were tribal, with no unified society or single Māori identity until after the arrival of Europeans. Nevertheless, common elements could be found in all Māori groups in pre-European New Zealand, including a shared Polynesian heritage, a common basic language, familial associations, traditions of warfare, and similar mythologies and religious beliefs.[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-167)
Most Māori lived in villages, which were inhabited by several _[whānau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wh%C4%81nau "Whānau")_ (extended families) who collectively formed a _[hapū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hap%C5%AB "Hapū")_ (clan or subtribe). Members of a _hapū_ cooperated with food production, gathering resources, raising families and defence. Māori society across New Zealand was broadly stratified into three classes of people: _[rangatira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangatira "Rangatira")_ , chiefs and ruling families; _tūtūā_ , commoners; and _mōkai_ , slaves. _[Tohunga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tohunga "Tohunga")_ also held special standing in their communities as specialists of revered arts, skills and esoteric knowledge.[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-King-p42p43-168)[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-TeAra-Taonui-169)
Shared ancestry, intermarriage and trade strengthened relationships between different groups. Many _hapū_ with mutually recognised shared ancestry formed _[iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi "Iwi")_ , or tribes, which were the largest social unit in Māori society. _Hapū_ and _iwi_ often united for expeditions to gather food and resources, or in times of conflict. In contrast, warfare developed as an integral part of traditional life, as different groups competed for food and resources, settled personal disputes, and sought to increase their prestige and authority.[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-King-p42p43-168)
[![Māori whānau from Rotorua in the 1880s](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Famille_Maori_1998-1361-139.jpg/330px-Famille_Maori_1998-1361-139.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Famille_Maori_1998-1361-139.jpg)Māori whānau from [Rotorua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotorua "Rotorua") in the 1880s
Early European settlers introduced tools, weapons, clothing and foods to Māori across New Zealand, in exchange for resources, land and labour. Māori began selectively adopting elements of Western society during the 19th century, including European clothing and food, and later Western education, religion and architecture.[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-170) However, as the 19th century wore on, relations between European colonial settlers and different Māori groups became increasingly strained. Tensions led to widespread conflict in the 1860s, and the [confiscation of millions of acres of Māori land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_land_confiscations "New Zealand land confiscations"). Significant amounts of land were also purchased by the colonial government and later through the [Native Land Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Land_Court "Native Land Court"). 
#### 20th century to present
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/The_M%C4%81ori%2C_Past_and_Present_-_Sydney_Mail_%281903%29.jpg/250px-The_M%C4%81ori%2C_Past_and_Present_-_Sydney_Mail_%281903%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_M%C4%81ori,_Past_and_Present_-_Sydney_Mail_\(1903\).jpg)"The Māori: Past and Present", article from _[The Sydney Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sydney_Mail "The Sydney Mail")_ , 1903
By the start of the 20th century, a greater awareness had emerged of a unified Māori identity, particularly in comparison to Pākehā, who now overwhelmingly outnumbered the Māori as a whole. Māori and Pākehā societies remained largely separate—socially, culturally, economically and geographically—for much of the 19th and early 20th centuries.[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-171) The key reason for this was that Māori remained almost exclusively a rural population, whereas increasingly the European population was urban especially after 1900. Nevertheless, Māori groups continued to engage with the government and in legal processes to increase their standing in (and ultimately further their incorporation into) wider New Zealand society.[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-172) The main point of contact with the government were the four Māori Members of Parliament. 
Many Māori [migrated to larger rural towns and cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation "Urbanisation") during the Depression and post-WWII periods in search of employment, leaving rural communities depleted and disconnecting many [urban Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_M%C4%81ori "Urban Māori") from their traditional social controls and tribal homelands. Yet while [standards of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standards_of_living "Standards of living") improved among Māori, they continued to lag behind Pākehā in areas such as health, income, skilled employment and access to higher levels of education. Māori leaders and government policymakers alike struggled to deal with [social issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_issues "Social issues") stemming from increased urban migration, including a shortage of housing and jobs, and a rise in urban crime, poverty and health problems.[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Sorrenson_1997,_pp_339%E2%80%9341-173)
In regards to housing, a 1961 census revealed significant differences in the living conditions of Māori and Europeans. That year, out of all the (unshared) non-Māori private dwellings in New Zealand, 96.8 per cent had a bath or shower, 94.1 per cent a hot water service, 88.7 per cent a flush toilet, 81.6 per cent a refrigerator, and 78.6 per cent an electric washing machine. By contrast, for all (unshared) Māori private dwellings that same year, 76.8 per cent had a bath or shower, 68.9 per cent a hot water service, 55.8 per cent a refrigerator, 54.1 per cent a flush toilet, and 47 per cent an electric washing machine.[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-174)
While the arrival of Europeans had a profound impact on the Māori way of life, many aspects of traditional society have survived into the 21st century. Māori participate fully in all spheres of New Zealand culture and society, leading largely Western lifestyles while also maintaining their own cultural and social customs. The traditional social strata of _rangatira_ , _tūtūā_ and _mōkai_ have all but disappeared from Māori society, while the roles of _tohunga_ and _[kaumātua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaum%C4%81tua "Kaumātua")_ are still present. Traditional kinship ties are also actively maintained, and the _whānau_ in particular remains an integral part of Māori life.[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-175)
###  _Marae_ , _hapū_ and _iwi_
[![Whenuakura Marae in Taranaki](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Whenuakura_Marae%2C_Taranaki%2C_New_Zealand.jpg/250px-Whenuakura_Marae%2C_Taranaki%2C_New_Zealand.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Whenuakura_Marae,_Taranaki,_New_Zealand.jpg)[Whenuakura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whenuakura "Whenuakura") Marae in Taranaki
Māori society at a local level is particularly visible at the _[marae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marae "Marae")_. Formerly the central meeting spaces in traditional villages, _marae_ today usually comprise a group of buildings around an open space, that frequently host events such as weddings, funerals, church services and other large gatherings, with traditional protocol and etiquette usually observed. They also serve as the base of one or sometimes several _hapū_.[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-176)
Most Māori affiliate with one or more _iwi_ (and _hapū_), based on genealogical descent (_[whakapapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whakapapa "Whakapapa")_). _Iwi_ vary in size, from a few hundred members to over 100,000 in the case of Ngāpuhi. Many people do not live in their traditional tribal regions as a result of urban migration (_see[Urban Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_M%C4%81ori "Urban Māori")_). _Iwi_ are usually governed by _[rūnanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C5%ABnanga "Rūnanga")_ (tribal councils or assemblies) which represent the _iwi_ in consultations and negotiations with the New Zealand government.[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-177)
### Race relations
See also: [Hori (slur)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hori_\(slur\) "Hori \(slur\)") and [New Zealanders § Race and ethnic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealanders#Race_and_ethnic_relations "New Zealanders")
[![Protest hikoi during the Foreshore and Seabed controversy in 2004](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Hikoi_011.jpg/250px-Hikoi_011.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hikoi_011.jpg)Protest _[hīkoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C4%ABkoi "Hīkoi")_ during the [foreshore and seabed controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_foreshore_and_seabed_controversy "New Zealand foreshore and seabed controversy") in 2004 [![New Zealand endorses Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous People, 2010](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/NZ_delegation_UN_Forum_on_Indigenous_Issues.jpg/330px-NZ_delegation_UN_Forum_on_Indigenous_Issues.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NZ_delegation_UN_Forum_on_Indigenous_Issues.jpg)New Zealand endorsed the United Nations [Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_on_the_Rights_of_Indigenous_Peoples "Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples") in April 2010.
Māori issues are a prominent feature of race relations in New Zealand. Historically, many Pākehā viewed race relations in their country as being the "best in the world", a view that prevailed until Māori urban migration in the mid-20th century brought cultural and socioeconomic differences to wider attention.[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-178)
Māori protest movements grew significantly in the 1960s and 1970s seeking redress for past grievances, particularly in regard to land rights. Successive governments have responded by enacting [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action "Affirmative action") programmes, funding cultural rejuvenation initiatives and negotiating tribal settlements for past breaches of the Treaty of Waitangi.[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-179) Further efforts have focused on reducing [socioeconomic disparity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality "Economic inequality").[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-180)
A 2007 [Department of Corrections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Corrections_\(New_Zealand\) "Department of Corrections \(New Zealand\)") report found that Māori are disproportionately represented in the criminal justice system: "a number of studies have shown evidence of greater likelihood, associated only with ethnicity, for Māori offenders to have police contact, be charged, lack legal representation, not be granted bail, plead guilty, be convicted, be sentenced to non-monetary penalties, and be denied release to Home Detention".[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-181) Conversely, critics denounce the scale of assistance given to Māori as amounting to preferential treatment for a select group of people based on race.[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-WaitangiDebateTVNZ-99) Both sentiments were highlighted during the [foreshore and seabed controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_foreshore_and_seabed_controversy "New Zealand foreshore and seabed controversy") in 2004, in which the New Zealand government claimed sole ownership of the New Zealand foreshore and seabed, over the objections of Māori groups who were seeking customary title.[[176]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-182)
## Socioeconomic issues
Māori on average have fewer assets than the rest of the population, and run greater risks of many negative economic and social outcomes. Over 50 per cent of Māori live in areas in the three highest deprivation deciles, compared with 24 per cent of the rest of the population.[[177]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-183)
Although Māori make up 16.5 per cent of the population,[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-184) they make up 53.0 per cent of the prison population.[[179]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-185) Māori have higher unemployment rates than other ethnic groups in New Zealand, which is believed to partially account for their over-representation in the criminal justice system; many young Māori, finding themselves unemployed, are picked up for alcohol-related behaviours or small crimes such as vandalism.[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-186) [Underemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underemployment "Underemployment") is in turn attributed to persistent [institutional racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_racism "Institutional racism") in New Zealand.[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-187)[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-188)
"Only 47 per cent of Māori school-leavers finish school with qualifications higher than [NCEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Certificate_of_Educational_Achievement "National Certificate of Educational Achievement") Level One; compared to 74 per cent European; 87 per cent Asian."[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-189) Although New Zealand rates very well globally in the PISA rankings that compare national performance in reading, science and maths, "once you disaggregate the PISA scores, Pakeha students are second in the world and Māori are 34th."[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-190) At the 2018 New Zealand census, 25.3% of Māori aged 15 and over had no formal qualifications, compared to 17.1% for non-Māori New Zealanders, and only 12.5% of Māori have a bachelor's degree or higher, compared to 26.8% of non-Māori.[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-191)
Also, a 2008 study by the New Zealand Family Violence Clearinghouse showed that Māori women and children are more likely to experience domestic violence than any other ethnic group.[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-192)
### Health
In 2017–2019, life expectancy for Māori in New Zealand was 73.4 years for males and 77.1 years for females, compared to 80.9 years for non-Māori males and 84.4 years for non-Māori females, a difference of 7.5 and 7.3 years respectively.[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-193)[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Daly-194) However, Māori have a wide range of life expectancies across regions: Māori living in [Marlborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlborough_District "Marlborough District") have the highest life expectancy at 79.9 years for males and 83.4 years for females, while Māori living in the [Gisborne District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gisborne_District "Gisborne District") have the lowest life expectancy at 71.2 years for males and 75.2 years for females.[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-Daly-194)
Māori suffer more health problems, including higher levels of alcohol and drug abuse, smoking and obesity. Less frequent use of healthcare services mean that late diagnosis and treatment intervention lead to higher levels of morbidity and mortality in many manageable conditions.[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-195)[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-196) Compared with non-Māori, Māori people experience higher rates of [heart disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease "Cardiovascular disease"), [strokes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stroke "Stroke"), most [cancers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cancer "Cancer"), [respiratory diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_disease "Respiratory disease"), [rheumatic fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rheumatic_fever "Rheumatic fever"), [suicide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide "Suicide") and [self-harm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-harm "Self-harm"), and [infant deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality "Infant mortality").[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-197)
In April 2021, [the government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Labour_Government_of_New_Zealand "Sixth Labour Government of New Zealand") announced the creation of the first dedicated Māori Health Authority "with the power to directly commission health services for Māori and to partner with Health NZ in other aspects of the health system".[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-198)
## Commerce
See also: [Cultural appropriation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_appropriation "Cultural appropriation")
Wider commercial exposure has increased public awareness of the Māori culture, but has also resulted in several legal disputes. Between 1998 and 2006, [Ngāti Toa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Toa "Ngāti Toa") attempted to trademark the _haka_ "_[Ka Mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka_Mate "Ka Mate")_ " to prevent its use by commercial organisations without their permission.[[193]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-199) In 2001, Danish toymaker Lego [faced legal action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bionicle#M%C4%81ori_language_controversy "Bionicle") by several Māori tribal groups opposed to them trademarking Māori words used in the Bionicle product range.[[194]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-200)
## Political representation
Main article: [Māori politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_politics "Māori politics")
[![The Māori Parliament at Pāpāwai, Greytown in 1897](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Papawai1897.jpg/330px-Papawai1897.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Papawai1897.jpg)The opening of the Māori Parliament at Pāpāwai, [Greytown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greytown,_New_Zealand "Greytown, New Zealand") in 1897, with [Richard Seddon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Seddon "Richard Seddon") in attendance [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Tino_Rangatiratanga_Maori_sovereignty_movement_flag.svg/250px-Tino_Rangatiratanga_Maori_sovereignty_movement_flag.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tino_Rangatiratanga_Maori_sovereignty_movement_flag.svg)The [national Māori flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_M%C4%81ori_flag "National Māori flag"), also known as the _[Tino rangatiratanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tino_rangatiratanga "Tino rangatiratanga")_ (absolute sovereignty) flag. Designed in 1989,[[195]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-201) it is widely used by Māori groups.
Māori have been represented to [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown "The Crown") in New Zealand politics since the [Declaration of the Independence of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Independence_of_New_Zealand "Declaration of the Independence of New Zealand"), before the Treaty of Waitangi was signed in 1840. Being a traditionally tribal people, no one organisation ostensibly speaks for all Māori nationwide. The [Māori King Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_King_Movement "Māori King Movement") (_Kīngitanga_) originated in the 1860s as an attempt by several iwi to unify under one leader; in modern times, it serves a largely ceremonial role. Another attempt at political unity was the Kotahitanga Movement, which established a separate Māori Parliament that held annual sessions from 1892 until its last sitting in 1902.[[196]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-202)
Māori have had [reserved seats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_electorates "Māori electorates") in the [New Zealand Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Parliament "New Zealand Parliament") since 1868.[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-seats-203) Māori received [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage "Universal suffrage") with other New Zealand citizens in 1893.[[198]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-204) Currently, Māori reserved electorates account for seven of the 120 seats in New Zealand's unicameral parliament, and consideration of and consultation with Māori have become routine requirements for councils and government organisations.[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-seats-203) The contesting of these seats was the first opportunity for many Māori to participate in New Zealand elections, although the elected Māori representatives initially struggled to assert significant influence. [Sir Āpirana Ngata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80pirana_Ngata "Āpirana Ngata") has often been described as the foremost Māori politician to have served in Parliament in the mid-20th century.[[199]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-205)
Debate occurs frequently as to the relevance and legitimacy of the separate [electoral roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_roll "Electoral roll") and the reserved seats. The National Party announced in 2008 it would abolish the seats when all historic Treaty settlements have been resolved, which it aimed to complete by 2014.[[200]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-206) However, after the election National reached an agreement with the Māori Party not to abolish the seats until Māori give their approval.[[201]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-207)
Several Māori political parties have formed over the years to improve the position of Māori in New Zealand society. The present [Māori Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Party "Māori Party"), formed in 2004, secured 1.32 per cent of the [party vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_vote "Party vote") at the [2014 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_New_Zealand_general_election "2014 New Zealand general election") and held two seats in the [51st New Zealand Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/51st_New_Zealand_Parliament "51st New Zealand Parliament"), with two MPs serving as Ministers outside [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Cabinet "New Zealand Cabinet"). The party did not achieve any representatives in the [52nd New Zealand Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/52nd_New_Zealand_Parliament "52nd New Zealand Parliament"),[[202]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-208) but regained two seats in the [53rd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/53rd_New_Zealand_Parliament "53rd New Zealand Parliament").[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-209)
Following the 2020 reelection of the [New Zealand Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Labour_Party "New Zealand Labour Party") to government, Labour Minister [Nanaia Mahuta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanaia_Mahuta "Nanaia Mahuta") became the first female Māori [Foreign Minister of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(New_Zealand\) "Minister of Foreign Affairs \(New Zealand\)"); she replaced [Winston Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Peters "Winston Peters"), also Māori, in the role. In 2016 she became the first Member of Parliament to have _moko kauae_ (the traditional Māori female facial tattoo).[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-210) In the 2020 election more MPs with _moko kauae_ entered Parliament, including Māori Party co-leader [Debbie Ngarewa-Packer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debbie_Ngarewa-Packer "Debbie Ngarewa-Packer") and [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_Aotearoa_New_Zealand "Green Party of Aotearoa New Zealand") MP [Elizabeth Kerekere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Kerekere "Elizabeth Kerekere"). 
The [54th Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/54th_New_Zealand_Parliament "54th New Zealand Parliament") after the [2023 New Zealand general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_general_election "2023 New Zealand general election") has seen a historically high number of Māori MPs at 33. Māori are at 27% of the parliament while in the general population they are 17%.[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-211)

## Notes
  1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_ref-14 "Jump up")** Māori: [[ˈmaːɔɾi]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/M%C4%81ori "Help:IPA/Māori") [](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/e/ec/Rar-M%C4%81ori.ogg/Rar-M%C4%81ori.ogg.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rar-M%C4%81ori.ogg "File:Rar-Māori.ogg"); New Zealand English: [/ˈmʌːori/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") or [/ˈmæʊɹiː/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English"); UK English: [/ˈmaʊri/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English").[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-6) Also spelled _Maori_[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-7)[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-8)[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-9)[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-10)[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-11) or, uncommonly, _Maaori_.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-12)[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-13)
  2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_ref-22 "Jump up")** _Māori_ has cognates in other [Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_languages "Polynesian languages"), such as [Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language "Hawaiian language") _maoli_ , [Tahitian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitian_language "Tahitian language") _mā'ohi_ , and [Cook Islands Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islands_M%C4%81ori "Cook Islands Māori") _māori_ , which all share similar meanings.
  3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_ref-23 "Jump up")** E.g. _[kanaka maoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanaka_maoli "Kanaka maoli")_ , meaning native [Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii "Hawaii"). (In the [Hawaiian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language "Hawaiian language"), the Polynesian letter "T" regularly becomes a "K", and the Polynesian letter "R" regularly becomes an "L".)
  4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_ref-26 "Jump up")** The orthographic conventions developed by the [Māori Language Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Language_Commission "Māori Language Commission") (_Te Taura Whiri i te Reo Māori_) recommend the use of the [macron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macron_\(diacritic\) "Macron \(diacritic\)") (ā ē ī ō ū) to denote long vowels. Contemporary English-language usage in New Zealand tends to avoid the [anglicised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicise "Anglicise") plural form of the word _Māori_ with an "s": The Māori language generally marks plurals by changing the [article](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_\(grammar\) "Article \(grammar\)") rather than the noun, for example: _te waka_ (the canoe); _ngā waka_ (the canoes).
  5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_ref-30 "Jump up")** In 2003, [Christian Cullen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Cullen "Christian Cullen") became a member of the [Māori rugby team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_M%C4%81ori_rugby_union_team "New Zealand Māori rugby union team") despite having, according to his father, about 1/64 Māori ancestry.[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-29)
  6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_ref-104 "Jump up")** Also spelled _Kiingitanga_. The preferred [orthography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthography "Orthography") of the [Waikato-Tainui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waikato-Tainui "Waikato-Tainui") [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi "Iwi") is to use doubled vowels rather than [macrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macron_\(diacritic\)#Vowel_length "Macron \(diacritic\)") to indicate long vowels.[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-102)[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people#cite_note-103)
